# Claim ledger: quadratics levels 1 to 3 (audit quadratics-1-3, round 1)

Stage 0, reduced depth: definitions, rules, "always / never / every / only" claims, conventions, and every graded key, wrong option and slip. Rows marked KEY, WRONG, SLIP, SPARE, MODEL, RUBRIC and METHOD come one per graded element of the JSON; DEF, RULE, ALWAYS, CLAIM, CONVENTION and CONTEXT rows are stated rules. Sources: Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra* (sections cited by title); Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 (chapters 1 to 4); the concepts script `docs/reports/quadratics-L1-3-concepts.py` (A: option identities, B: keys and slips, C: fluency sweeps, D: canvas points).

Rows: 1164. Verdicts: correct 1154, wrong 2, imprecise 7, misleading 1, unverified 0.

| l11-meeting-the-axis | summary.ideas[1] | RULE: The line of symmetry is halfway between the roots, at $x = \tfrac{p + q}{2}$, and the vertex sits on it. | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.4 (roots, y-intercept, line of symmetry) |

| l13-best-price | summary.ideas[0] | RULE: Revenue is price times quantity. With linear demand $q = A - Bp$ it is the quadratic $p(A - Bp)$. | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.6 (modelling with quadratics); Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "Maximum of the product" |
| l13-best-price | summary.ideas[1] | RULE: The best price is the vertex, halfway between £0.00 and the price where demand vanishes: $\tfrac{A}{2B}$. | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.6 (modelling with quadratics); Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "Maximum of the product" |
| l13-best-price | summary.ideas[2] | RULE: A unit cost moves one root, so the best price rises by half the cost, not all of it. | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.6 (modelling with quadratics); Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "Maximum of the product" |
| l13-best-price | summary.formula | RULE: Revenue-maximising price: p^* = \dfrac{A}{2B} (With a unit cost $c$: $p^* = \tfrac{1}{2}\left(c + \tfrac{A}{B}\right)$.) | correct | identity checked with sympy; Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.6 (modelling with quadratics); Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "Maximum of the product" |
| l13-best-price | summary.trap | RULE: Adding the full cost to the old best price overshoots. The peak moves by half the cost. | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.6 (modelling with quadratics); Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "Maximum of the product" |

| homework-3 | items[9].model | MODEL: **Profit.** Each client pays $p$ and costs 5, so each earns $p - 5$: $$\begin{aligned} \pi(p) &= (p - 5)(2000 - 80p) \\ &= -80p^2 + 2400p - 10… | correct | every line recomputed (script B identities; hand); Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.4, 2.6 and 3.5 |
| homework-3 | items[9].rubric[0] | RUBRIC: Writes profit as $(p - 5)(2000 - 80p)$ and expands it to $-80p^2 + 2400p - 10000$. | correct | matches the model answer |
| homework-3 | items[9].rubric[1] | RUBRIC: Completes the square correctly to $-80(p - 15)^2 + 8000$, with a reason for each line. | correct | matches the model answer |
| homework-3 | items[9].rubric[2] | RUBRIC: Explains the maximum: the square term is never positive because $a = -80 < 0$. | correct | matches the model answer |
| homework-3 | items[9].rubric[3] | RUBRIC: States the best price £15.00 and the greatest profit £8,000.00 a month. | correct | matches the model answer |
| homework-3 | items[9].rubric[4] | RUBRIC: Finds the revenue-maximising price £12.50 from the roots 0 and 25, or from the vertex rule. | correct | matches the model answer |
| homework-3 | items[9].rubric[5] | RUBRIC: Explains that the cost moves one root by £5.00, so the midpoint moves by £2.50. | correct | matches the model answer |

| l1-two-brackets | screens[2].prose | DEF expanding: rewriting a product of brackets as a sum of separate terms | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 1.2 |
| l1-two-brackets | screens[6].explain | ALWAYS: a squared bracket always has a middle term, twice the number times x | correct | (x + a)^2 = x^2 + 2ax + a^2; the term vanishes only for a = 0, which is not a bracket with a number. Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "The square of a sum formula" |
| l1-two-brackets | screens[7].explain | RULE: in (x + a)(x + b) the middle number is a + b and the last is ab | correct | distributive law, sympy |
| l1-two-brackets | screens[8].prose | CONVENTION: £2,000 for two years at rate r, interest added each year, grows to 2000(1 + r)^2; the r^2 piece is interest on interest | correct | annual compounding; 2000 x 1.04^2 = 2163.20 (script B) |
| l1-two-brackets | quiz[2].explain | CLAIM: averaging (r - m)^2 = r^2 - 2mr + m^2 over the returns gives the variance shortcut | correct | derivation in homework-1 items[7]; population variance, stated there |
| l2-product-and-sum | screens[1].canvas.state.caption | EVERY: "Every pair of whole numbers that multiplies to 24" (four positive pairs listed) | imprecise | finding 5: under the app's own usage (equations l10: whole numbers include negatives) there are eight pairs; screens[4] of this lesson lists the negative pairs for 20. "positive" is missing |
| l2-product-and-sum | screens[2].prose | DEF factorising: turning x^2 + 10x + 24 back into (x + 4)(x + 6) | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 1.3 |
| l2-product-and-sum | screens[3].prose | RULE: product -15, so exactly one of the two numbers is negative | correct | sign rule for a product of two reals |
| l2-product-and-sum | screens[4].explain | RULE: a positive last term means the two numbers share a sign, the sign of the middle term | correct | pq > 0 gives equal signs; p + q then has that sign |
| l2-product-and-sum | screens[5].explain | DEF perfect square: x^2 + 10x + 25 = (x + 5)^2, middle number twice 5 | correct | Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "The square of a sum formula" |
| l2-product-and-sum | screens[7].prose | RULE: x^2 + bx + c = (x + p)(x + q) when pq = c and p + q = b | correct | Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "Vieta's theorem" (with roots -p, -q); sympy |
| l2-product-and-sum | screens[8].explain | CLAIM: profit -(q - 3)(q - 9) is zero at 3 and 9 and positive in between | correct | sign table; table rows recomputed (script B) |
| l3-leading-coefficient | screens[2].prose | DEF/RULE splitting the middle term: two numbers with product ac and sum b | correct | proved on screens[7]: ps x qr = pr x qs = ac; Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 1.3 |
| l3-leading-coefficient | screens[5].explain | CONVENTION fully factorised: (2x + 4)(x + 3) is equal but not fully factorised | correct | standard usage: no bracket keeps a common numerical factor |
| l3-leading-coefficient | screens[7].explain | ALWAYS: the right split always exists when the quadratic factorises | correct | if ax^2 + bx + c = (px + q)(rx + s) then ps and qr have sum b and product ac |
| l4-difference-of-squares | screens[2].explain | DEF/ALWAYS difference of two squares: it always factorises into a sum times a difference | correct | Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "The difference of squares formula": a^2 - b^2 = (a + b)(a - b) |
| l4-difference-of-squares | screens[4].explain | RULE: the rule works for any two squares, not only x^2 | correct | identity in a and b |
| l4-difference-of-squares | screens[6].explain | RULE: "we need two numbers with product 9 and sum 0, and none exist. A difference of squares factorises; a sum of squares does not." | imprecise | finding 7: no two real numbers (same sign, so the sum is not 0). Over the complex numbers 3i and -3i work. The restriction is unstated here, while l8 is careful to say "no real" |
| l4-difference-of-squares | screens[8].explain | ALWAYS: up r then down r always loses r^2 of the value; volatility costs money even with no trend | correct | (1 + r)(1 - r) = 1 - r^2 < 1 for r not 0; 50 x 0.9936 = 49.68 (script B) |
| l5-product-of-zero | screens[1].explain | DEF roots: the values of x that make the quadratic zero, where the curve meets the x-axis | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.1 and 2.4 |
| l5-product-of-zero | screens[2].prose | RULE: a product is zero when one of its factors is zero; write "or", not "and" | correct | zero product property of the reals |
| l5-product-of-zero | screens[5].explain | DEF repeated root: (x - 3)^2 = 0 has one solution; the curve touches the axis instead of crossing | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.5 |
| l5-product-of-zero | screens[6].explain | RULE: dividing both sides by x hides the root x = 0, because you can never divide by 0 | correct | x^2 = 5x has roots 0 and 5 (sympy) |
| l5-product-of-zero | screens[7].prose | RULE: AB = 0 if and only if A = 0 or B = 0; only 0 has this property | correct | if AB = 0 and A is not 0, divide by A; for k not 0, AB = k fixes neither factor |
| l5-product-of-zero | summary.ideas[2] | NEVER: never divide both sides by x: it throws away the root x = 0 | correct | if both sides are multiples of x then x = 0 is a root, so the division always loses it |
| l6-completing-the-square | screens[2].prose | DEF completing the square: x^2 + 8x = (x + 4)^2 - 16 | correct | Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "Completing the square"; Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.2 |
| l6-completing-the-square | screens[4].explain | CONVENTION: x + 3 = ±2 means x + 3 = 2 or x + 3 = -2 | correct | standard |
| l6-completing-the-square | screens[6].explain | ALWAYS: always write ± when you square-root an equation | correct | u^2 = k (k > 0) has the two roots ±sqrt(k) |
| l6-completing-the-square | screens[7].prose | RULE: x^2 + bx = (x + b/2)^2 - (b/2)^2; the corner is always positive | correct | sympy identity; (b/2)^2 > 0 for b not 0 |
| l6-completing-the-square | screens[8].explain | RULE: a square is never negative, so P = 64 - (q - 10)^2 is largest at q = 10 | correct | script B |
| l6-completing-the-square | quiz[1].explain | CONTEXT: the root 1 + r = -1.1 means r = -210%, rejected | correct | r = -2.1; a loss above 100% is impossible for the investment |
| l6-completing-the-square | quiz[3].explain | RULE: (x + 2)^2 = -3 has no real solution; the curve stays above the axis | correct | "real" is stated; Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.5 |
| l7-the-formula | screens[0].prose | CLAIM: no two whole numbers multiply to -1 and add to 4, so x^2 + 4x - 1 will not factorise, yet it has two roots | correct | convention: factorise means whole-number brackets (as in all of level 1); the sentence itself says the roots exist. Pairs (1, -1), (-1, 1) add to 0 |
| l7-the-formula | screens[0].explain | DEF surd: a root such as sqrt(5), left unrounded, is exact | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 1.5 |
| l7-the-formula | screens[1].explain | RULE: the roots are a centre -b/2 plus or minus a spread | correct | completed square with a = 1 |
| l7-the-formula | screens[4].explain | RULE: sqrt(4a^2) = 2a, and the ± covers either sign of a | correct | sqrt(4a^2) = 2\|a\|; ±2\|a\| and ±2a are the same pair. The remark is right and needed |
| l7-the-formula | screens[7].prose | EVERY: every quadratic yields to one formula x = (-b ± sqrt(b^2 - 4ac)) / 2a | correct | Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "The formula for quadratic equation"; the case of a negative discriminant is taken up in l8, as the explain says |
| l8-how-many-solutions | screens[0].explain | RULE: a square is never negative, so (x - 2)^2 = -1 has no real solution | correct | "real" stated in feedback and explain |
| l8-how-many-solutions | screens[2].prose | DEF discriminant: b^2 - 4ac, the part under the square root | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.5 |
| l8-how-many-solutions | screens[7].explain | ALWAYS: with a and c of opposite signs two roots are guaranteed; b^2 is never negative | correct | ac < 0 gives b^2 - 4ac > 0; swept on a grid (script B) |
| l8-how-many-solutions | screens[8].prose | RULE: discriminant > 0 two roots, = 0 one root, < 0 no real roots | correct | swept over 648 integer triples with sympy real_roots (script B); Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.5 |
| l8-how-many-solutions | summary.formula.note | RULE: "Below 0: none" | correct | short for no real roots; the idea line on the same card says "no real roots" |
| l8-how-many-solutions | screens[9].explain | CLAIM: the single touch at T = 625 is the revenue-maximising volume q = 25 | correct | vertex of q(50 - q) |
| homework-2 | items[4].explain | RULE: the roots add to -b/a; the centre -b/2a is half of this | correct | Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "Vieta's theorem" |
| homework-2 | items[5].feedback.byKey.neg | CONTEXT: 3 - sqrt(13) is negative, before entry, rejected | correct | domain t >= 0 |
| problems-2 | items[0].parts[1].explain | CONTEXT: t = -1 solves the equation but is before the launch | correct | domain t >= 0; 5(t - 3)(t + 1) (sympy) |
| problems-2 | items[2].parts[0].feedback | CONTEXT: n = -16 rejected, rows cannot be negative | correct | (n + 16)(n - 15) |
| problems-2 | items[2].prose | RULE: a triangle stack of n rows holds n(n + 1)/2 cans | correct | sum of 1 to n |
| l9-shape-of-a-square | screens[1].explain | RULE: positive a opens upwards, negative a downwards; at a = 0 a straight line; bigger \|a\| is narrower | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.4 |
| l9-shape-of-a-square | screens[2].prose | DEF parabola and line of symmetry ("every curve you made is a parabola") | correct | the a = 0 exception is stated in the explain just before and in the summary note |
| l9-shape-of-a-square | screens[6].explain | RULE: a plus inside the bracket moves the curve left, a minus moves it right | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 4.5 (translations) |
| l9-shape-of-a-square | screens[8].prose | RULE: y = a(x - h)^2 + k: a > 0 opens upwards, h moves right, k moves up, line of symmetry x = h | correct | proved on screens[7] |
| l9-shape-of-a-square | screens[9].explain | CLAIM: "the curve opens upwards, so big moves either way help the holder: this is convexity" | misleading | finding 6: P(+2) = 87.20 < 100; a yield rise still loses money, and P(d) < 100 for 0 < d < 23.3. Convexity adds 0.3d^2 relative to the straight line; it does not make a rise in yields a gain. Hull, *Options, Futures and Other Derivatives*, ch. 4 (duration and convexity) |
| l10-turning-point | screens[2].prose | DEF vertex / turning point and completed square form: vertex (h, k) | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.4 |
| l10-turning-point | screens[3].explain | RULE: a < 0 the vertex is a maximum, a > 0 a minimum | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 2.4 |
| l10-turning-point | screens[7].explain | RULE: vertex at x = -b/2a with height k = c - b^2/4a | correct | sympy: a(x + b/2a)^2 + c - b^2/4a expands to ax^2 + bx + c |
| l10-turning-point | quiz[3].explain | ONLY: the vertex is the y-intercept only when b = 0 | correct | -b/2a = 0 iff b = 0 |
| l11-meeting-the-axis | screens[2].prose | DEF y-intercept: the height at x = 0; y = a(x - p)(x - q) meets the y-axis at apq | correct | substitute x = 0 |
| l11-meeting-the-axis | screens[1].explain | RULE: the line of symmetry is at the average of the roots; when p = q the curve just touches | correct | proved on screens[7] |
| l11-meeting-the-axis | summary.ideas[2] | RULE: two roots, one repeated root or none: vertex below, on or above the axis (for a > 0) | correct | k = -(b^2 - 4ac)/4a |
| l11-meeting-the-axis | screens[9].explain | CLAIM: straddle model 0.05x^2 - 1.80 loses most with no move and makes money beyond £6.00 either way | correct | roots ±6 (script B) |
| l12-above-or-below | screens[0].explain | RULE: x^2 - 4 < 0 exactly when -2 < x < 2; the ends are left out | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 3.5 |
| l12-above-or-below | screens[2].prose | DEF intersections: points where both equations hold at once | correct | Pearson Edexcel AS and A level Pure Mathematics Year 1 3.3 |
| l12-above-or-below | screens[6].explain | RULE: x^2 > 9 holds outside the roots; taking a square root loses the negative side | correct | Gelfand and Shen, *Algebra*, "Quadratic inequalities" |
